Inhibiting CDK5 and HPV18 E6 Interaction Suppresses Cervical Cancer Cell Growth - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A study published in 2026 found that inhibiting the interaction between host cyclin-dependent kinase 5 (CDK5) and HPV18 E6 oncoprotein suppresses cervical cancer cell growth. The research demonstrated that CDK5 and HPV18 E6 form stable complexes, with CDK5 stabilizing HPV18 E6 levels, potentially leading to increased viral oncoprotein levels and cancer cell proliferation. This suggests a potential therapeutic target for HPV-positive cervical cancer, but further investigation and clinical trials are needed to confirm these findings.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, November 28, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Cancer Inhibiting CDK5 and HPV18 E6 Interaction Suppresses Cervical Cancer Cell Growth by November 23, 2026 in Cancer Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 Inhibiting CDK5 and HPV18 E6 Interaction Suppresses Cervical Cancer Cell Growth Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Persistent infection by high-risk types of human papillomavirus represents the primary causative factor in cervical cancer, which ranks as the fourth most common cancer diagnosed among women globally. Although regular clinical screening and surgical intervention provide reliable disease prevention in regions with developed health infrastructure, the overall global burden has remained high, particularly across low- and middle-income countries where financial resources and vaccination access continue to be constrained. Among more than four hundred identified viral genotypes, thirteen are classified as high-risk variants, with types 16 and 18 together responsible for more than seventy percent of all invasive cervical malignancies. During persistent infection and neoplastic progression, viral DNA integrates into the human genome, resulting in sustained overexpression of viral early oncoproteins designated E6 and E7. Historically, research has emphasized the capacity of E6 to recruit the ubiquitin ligase E6-associated protein to degrade the host tumor suppressor p53, while E7 targets the retinoblastoma protein pRB. However, identifying host cellular factors that directly bind viral oncoproteins to reinforce their stability represents a critical frontier for targeted oncology. In a newly reported study, investigators explored how host cyclin-dependent kinase 5, an enzyme traditionally characterized for its multifunctional regulatory roles in neuronal migration, survival, and differentiation within the central nervous system, interfaces with high-risk human papillomavirus oncoproteins. While earlier broad gene expression analyses drawn from public cancer databases demonstrated increased messenger RNA levels of the kinase in cervical squamous cell carcinoma and ovarian cancers compared to normal tissue, these repositories did not stratify samples by viral status. To resolve this question experimentally, the researchers examined baseline protein abundance across a panel of cultured human cell lines. Immunoblotting demonstrated that the kinase was expressed at significantly higher levels in human papillomavirus-positive cervical cancer models, including HeLa cells expressing type 18 and CaSki cells harboring type 16, relative to virus-null controls such as immortalized HGK12 primary gingival keratinocytes and tongue squamous cell carcinoma lines SAS and UPCI:SCC025. This observation provided direct initial evidence that high-risk viral oncoproteins correspond with elevated host kinase expression. To investigate whether a physical complex forms between the host enzyme and viral factors, researchers conducted in vitro protein-protein interaction assays. Scansite computational modeling first predicted that human papillomavirus E6 contains potential consensus motif sequences recognized by the kinase. Subsequent glutathione S-transferase pull-down experiments and co-immunoprecipitation assays using transfected human embryonic kidney 293 cell extracts confirmed that the host kinase forms stable physical complexes with both viral proteins. Quantitative measurements revealed that the kinase bound significantly more readily to E6 than to E7, displaying a marked preference for type 18 E6 over type 16 E6, with relative binding values measured at 1.343 ± 0.04749 compared to 0.8133 ± 0.02427. Immunofluorescence microscopy performed in virus-negative osteosarcoma U-2 OS cells further confirmed that the kinase and viral E6 proteins colocalize predominantly within the nuclear compartment. This nuclear assembly took place without altering the natural subcellular distribution of the host enzyme, establishing that the interaction represents an authentic intracellular event occurring inside the cell nucleus. Discover more cancer cancers Find Cancer Care Cancer Study Biology Further biochemical experiments revealed a reciprocal, mutualistic relationship governing the abundance of both proteins within the cell. Transient gene expression experiments in embryonic kidney cells using escalating plasmid ratios demonstrated that increasing kinase transfection from one to four micrograms elevated type 18 E6 protein levels by 5.25 ± 0.98-fold at a four-to-two ratio. Conversely, increasing viral oncoprotein expression elevated kinase abundance by 1.78 ± 0.23-fold at a one-to-two ratio and by 2.18 ± 0.13-fold at an equal ratio. Similar results were documented in cervical cancer cells and virus-negative head and neck cancer cells. Furthermore, selective short interfering RNA knockdown targeting viral oncoproteins in HeLa cells caused a marked drop in host kinase levels down to 0.67 ± 0.11 relative to control, accompanied by a robust 6.21 ± 1.20-fold rescue of the host tumor suppressor p53. In addition, an immortalized human keratinocyte line stably engineered to express the full-length type 18 genome exhibited pronounced elevations in both total and phosphorylated active kinase forms compared to parental controls, indicating that viral expression actively stimulates host kinase activity. To establish how the host kinase sustains elevated viral oncoprotein levels, the authors conducted cycloheximide chase assays measuring protein half-life and turnover kinetics over two hours. When human embryonic kidney cells expressed type 18 E6 alone, the viral protein decayed with a half-life of 54.24 minutes. Co-expression of the host kinase dramatically extended this survival, prolonging the viral protein half-life beyond 120 minutes. A parallel stabilization was noted for type 16 E6, whose half-life rose from 52.22 minutes to 120 minutes in the presence of the kinase. Notably, kinase activity profiling utilizing bioluminescent ADP-Glo kinase assays revealed that the host enzyme does not significantly phosphorylate the viral oncoprotein, demonstrating that the kinase does not utilize the viral protein as an enzymatic substrate. Instead, the experimental evidence indicates that physical non-covalent association outside the primary kinase domain shields the viral factor from degradation, sustaining steady-state oncoprotein levels through structural stabilization rather than direct biochemical phosphorylation. Discover more Study Chemistry Cancer Cell cancer cells Having established the functional significance of the kinase-oncoprotein interaction, the investigators conducted a multistage virtual and biological screen to identify small molecules capable of disrupting the protein complex. By filtering a structural chemical library of one thousand diverse compounds collected from PubChem, DrugBank, ZINC, and ChemSpider using molecular docking algorithms, ten promising candidates were advanced to biological testing. Cell counting kit-8 viability assays identified the small molecule designated CP681301 as a highly selective inhibitor of virus-positive cancer cells. In comparative dosage assessments, the compound displayed a half-maximal inhibitory concentration of 2.0 micromolar against type 18-positive HeLa cells and 3.7 micromolar against type 16-positive CaSki cells. In stark contrast, virus-negative C33A cervical cancer cells exhibited resistance, with inhibitory concentration values exceeding 10 micromolar. Longitudinal tracking over four days of 1.5 micromolar compound exposure maintained durable suppression of proliferation in HeLa and CaSki cells while showing no detectable growth inhibition in virus-negative controls. Direct biophysical characterization using microscale thermophoresis validated that the compound engages the target proteins with distinct binding affinities. The equilibrium dissociation constant of CP681301 was measured at 36 micromolar for purified type 18 E6 and 112 micromolar for type 16 E6, both showing higher affinity than its binding to the host kinase itself, which registered at 127 micromolar. In competitive pull-down assays, incubating purified components with 1.5 micromolar of the inhibitor significantly attenuated binding between the host kinase and type 18 E6, reducing relative protein association by 0.4600 ± 0.09-fold. Analysis of concentration-dependent cellular responses yielded median effective concentrations of 0.45 micromolar for oncoprotein reduction and 1.14 micromolar for kinase reduction in cultured HeLa cells. Quantitative polymerase chain reaction further verified that high-dose inhibitor exposure suppressed kinase messenger RNA in both positive lines and lowered viral oncoprotein transcripts in type 18-positive cells, indicating that transcriptional repression accompanies the observed protein loss. Functional cellular assays demonstrated that disrupting the host-viral complex profoundly diminished hallmark malignant phenotypes in cell culture models. In wound healing scratch assays, exposure to 0.6 and 1.5 micromolar concentrations of the inhibitor significantly restricted cell migration over twenty-four hours in both HeLa and CaSki lines. Anchorage-independent growth in soft agar was similarly curtailed: vehicle-treated HeLa cells generated robust colonies, whereas cells exposed to 1.5 micromolar compound produced only 69.00 ± 27.03 colonies, reflecting a statistically significant loss of transformation capacity. Transwell chamber invasion assays through extracellular matrix Matrigel demonstrated comparable dose-dependent reductions; relative to vehicle controls, HeLa cell invasion dropped to 230.00 ± 66.73 at 0.6 micromolar and fell to 65.00 ± 41.82 at 1.5 micromolar. Identical suppressive effects occurred in type 16 cells, whereas virus-negative C33A cells experienced no significant reduction in migratory, colonizing, or invasive behaviors under identical treatment conditions. At the mechanistic level, the inhibitor dismantled the oncogenic degradation cascade that normally eliminates host tumor suppressors. Cycloheximide chase measurements in HeLa cells revealed that 0.6 micromolar inhibitor treatment accelerated oncoprotein decay, reducing its cellular half-life from 71.97 minutes in control conditions to just 21.96 minutes, representing a more than threefold destabilization. Western blotting confirmed that pharmacological treatment caused simultaneous, dose-dependent depletion of endogenous kinase, viral E6, and the ubiquitin ligase E6AP over twenty-four hours. Biochemical pull-down experiments utilizing purified proteins demonstrated that 1.5 micromolar of the compound physically disrupted complex formation between type 18 E6 and the E6AP enzyme. By destabilizing the viral oncoprotein and preventing assembly of the tripartite degradation machinery, treatment effectively rescued the p53 protein from proteasomal destruction, restoring endogenous levels of this critical tumor suppressor in virus-positive cervical cancer cells without modifying p53 or E6AP levels in virus-negative control cells. Despite these promising cellular outcomes, the authors outlined key technical and biological limitations that restrict the immediate clinical translation of their findings. All experimental observations were derived from established in vitro cell cultures, and the investigations relied primarily on C33A as the single comparative virus-negative cervical cancer line, which may not encompass the full genomic diversity of human malignancies. The research team also lacked stable genomic knockdown cell models for the kinase and viral oncoprotein in HeLa cells, preventing evaluation of the permanent consequences of chronic complex disruption. Furthermore, because CP681301 exhibited comparable baseline growth suppression against certain virus-negative head and neck cancer cells and reduced E6AP across varied cellular backgrounds, the molecule possesses inherent cytotoxic liabilities and potential off-target effects. The authors concluded that while the host-viral interaction represents a validated therapeutic vulnerability, future drug development must design optimized pharmacological agents with enhanced structural specificity to safely disrupt this target across human papillomavirus-driven malignancies. 天然 HPV16 衣壳变体对标准 疫苗 的敏感性降低

Natural HPV16 Capsid Variants Show Reduced Sensitivity to Standard Vaccines
新闻简报发表在《肿瘤病毒研究》上的一项实验室研究发现,天然存在的 HPV16 变体的主要衣壳蛋白 L1 中的特定氨基酸替换可以降低这些变体对标准 HPV 疫苗 的敏感性。虽然几种自然循环的 HPV16 变体正常组装并有效感染人上皮细胞系,但特定变体对 疫苗 介导的抗体中和的敏感性显着降低。研究人员得出结论,自然循环的 L1 变体的持续分子 监测 对于预测持续的进化分歧并告知下一代广谱 疫苗 的组成至关重要。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该实验研究发表在《肿瘤病毒研究》杂志上,系统地研究了 HPV16 L1 蛋白的自然遗传多样性如何影响假病毒颗粒的物理形成、这些颗粒感染 宿主 细胞的能力,以及它们对三种标准预防药物 疫苗(二价 Cervarix、四价 Gardasil 和非价 Gardasil-9 制剂)引起的血清抗体的脆弱性。
地点与对象
所提供的证据中未报告。
事件
一项实验室研究表明,天然存在的 HPV16 变体的主要衣壳蛋白 L1 中的特定氨基酸取代可以降低这些变体对标准 HPV 疫苗 的敏感性。
规模、影响与风险
研究发现,虽然几种自然循环的 HPV16 变体正常组装并有效感染人类上皮细胞系,但由于抗体-抗原结合亲合力减弱,特定变体对 疫苗 介导的抗体中和的敏感性显着降低。高危型HPV 型(尤其是 HPV16)的持续感染是导致 宫颈癌 的主要原因,宫颈癌 是全球女性中第四大常见癌症。
应对、状态与不确定性
研究人员得出结论,自然循环的 L1 变体的持续分子 监测 对于预测持续的进化分歧并告知下一代广谱 疫苗 的组成至关重要。目前情况正在调查中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Natural HPV16 Capsid Variants Show Reduced Sensitivity to Standard Vaccines

News Brief
A laboratory study published in Tumour Virus Research found that specific amino acid substitutions in the major capsid protein L1 of naturally occurring HPV16 variants can reduce the sensitivity of these variants to standard HPV vaccines. While several naturally circulating HPV16 variants assemble normally and infect human epithelial cell lines efficiently, specific variants exhibit substantial reductions in susceptibility to vaccine-mediated antibody neutralization. The researchers concluded that sustained molecular surveillance of naturally circulating L1 variants is vital to anticipate ongoing evolutionary divergence and inform the composition of next-generation broad-spectrum vaccines.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, November 28, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Cancer Natural HPV16 Capsid Variants Show Reduced Sensitivity to Standard Vaccines by November 24, 2026 in Cancer Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 Natural HPV16 Capsid Variants Show Reduced Sensitivity to Standard Vaccines Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A comprehensive laboratory evaluation of naturally occurring variants of human papillomavirus type 16 has demonstrated that specific amino acid substitutions in the major capsid protein L1 can destabilize virion assembly and significantly diminish neutralization by vaccine-induced human antibodies. Published in the journal Tumour Virus Research, the experimental study systematically examined how the natural genetic diversification of the HPV16 L1 protein influences the physical formation of pseudovirus particles, the capacity of those particles to infect host cells, and their vulnerability to serum antibodies elicited by three standard prophylactic vaccines: the bivalent Cervarix, the quadrivalent Gardasil, and the nonavalent Gardasil-9 formulations. By combining computational structural modeling, in vitro pseudovirus assays, molecular dynamics simulations, and phylogenetic selection analyses, the research team established that while several naturally circulating variants assemble normally and infect human epithelial cell lines efficiently, specific variants exhibit substantial reductions in susceptibility to vaccine-mediated antibody neutralization, driven by weakened antibody-antigen binding avidity. Persistent infection with high-risk human papillomavirus types represents the primary etiological contributor to multiple malignancies, posing a profound global threat to women’s health. Cervical cancer ranks as the fourth most common cancer among women worldwide, with the World Health Organization recording an estimated 660,000 incident cases and 348,000 fatalities in 2022. Among high-risk types, HPV16 is responsible for more than fifty percent of all cervical cancer cases globally. Current prophylactic HPV vaccines are constructed using recombinant virus-like particles composed of the major capsid protein L1, which naturally self-assembles into pentameric capsomeres that form the outer virion shell. Although the double-stranded DNA virus evolves at a relatively constant rate and the L1 protein is considered relatively conserved, natural sequence variations can alter capsomere architecture, viral infectivity, and surface loop conformations. Because the principal neutralizing epitopes recognized by host antibodies reside within five surface-exposed hypervariable loops designated as BC, DE, EF, FG, and HI, amino acid substitutions within these regions have the potential to compromise antibody recognition and permit immune evasion. To investigate the extent of natural variation across circulating viral lineages, the investigators retrieved 1,437 complete or near-complete HPV16 L1 protein sequences with completeness exceeding 85 percent from the National Center for Biotechnology Information database. Using CD-HIT clustering to remove redundant entries, they consolidated the repository into 228 unique L1 amino acid sequences, which were subsequently aligned using the Clustal Omega algorithm implemented within UGENE software. Maximum likelihood phylogenetic reconstruction executed with IQ-TREE categorized these 228 unique sequences into four major evolutionary clades comprising fourteen distinct clusters. The consensus amino acid sequence derived from each cluster was confirmed to exist naturally in circulating populations. The consensus sequence matching accession P03101 in the UniProt database was designated as the reference wild-type construct, P16_WT, representing the most prevalent lineage at 49.8 percent of analyzed sequences. The remaining thirteen clusters, designated P16_1 through P16_13, ranged in natural prevalence from 0.07 percent to substantial frequencies, displaying branched divergence around the reference wild type across the phylogenetic tree. Comparative sequence analysis between the wild-type reference and the thirteen variant consensus sequences revealed a total of thirty distinct amino acid substitution sites distributed throughout the length of the L1 polypeptide chain. Structural mapping onto the assembled pentameric capsomere demonstrated that ten of these mutated residues mapped directly onto surface-exposed neutralizing epitope loops, exhibiting particular concentration across the EF, FG, and HI loops, whereas the BC and DE loops remained comparatively invariant. The remaining twenty mutation sites localized within relatively conserved structural framework regions of the viral capsomere. To evaluate the biophysical consequences of these mutations prior to laboratory synthesis, the researchers performed FoldX energy calculations across crystallographic datasets of L1 monomers, pentamers, and antibody-bound complexes. The free energy calculations predicted that variants P16_2, P16_3, P16_4, and P16_8 possessed positive free energy changes relative to wild type, indicating thermodynamic destabilization of the monomer or pentameric assemblies, whereas most predictions for P16_5 and P16_6 also indicated elevated free energy, while the remaining variants exhibited negative free energy shifts suggestive of maintained or enhanced structural stability. To empirically test particle assembly and infectivity, the authors synthesized codon-optimized variant L1 genes and inserted them into modified mammalian expression vectors along with the wild-type L2 gene and a green fluorescent protein reporter plasmid, cotransfecting human embryonic kidney 293FT cells to generate pseudovirions. Consistent with the biophysical FoldX predictions, variants P16_2, P16_3, P16_4, and P16_8 completely failed to assemble into infectious pseudovirus particles, generating zero detectable viral titers and revealing no recognizable capsids under negative-stain transmission electron microscopy. Variant P16_1 successfully assembled but yielded an extremely suppressed pseudovirus titer. Western blot analysis of whole-cell lysates conducted before and after a twenty-four-hour maturation period in hypertonic sodium chloride revealed that although all variants produced L1 protein to varying degrees before maturation, L1 protein in the four non-forming variants and P16_1 was undetectable or sharply diminished following maturation. In contrast, the remaining eight variants and the reference wild type assembled high-titer pseudoviruses with intact morphology, showing equivalent packaging capacity and demonstrating indistinguishable infectivity when titrated onto human HeLa and 293FT epithelial cultures. The eight successfully assembled high-titer variants along with the wild-type reference were subsequently evaluated in pseudovirus-based neutralization assays using human sera collected from individuals under fifty years of age who had completed standard three-dose immunization regimens with Cervarix (27 participants), Gardasil (26 participants), or Gardasil-9 (29 participants). Blood samples were drawn primarily within one month following the final vaccine dose. Neutralization titers against the reference wild-type strain showed slight numerical variations across the three vaccine cohorts, but these differences did not reach statistical significance, verifying that all three vaccines established robust baseline neutralizing titers against the standard HPV16 L1 antigen. However, when tested against the natural variants, marked differences in neutralization susceptibility emerged across the vaccine cohorts, uncovering distinct patterns of antibody escape and resilience that depended on both the specific viral mutational profile and the valency of the vaccine administered. Neutralizing titers against the assembled variants demonstrated that Cervarix-induced antibodies experienced significant reductions in neutralization efficacy against four variants: P16_5, P16_11, P16_12, and P16_13. The loss of inhibitory potency was most pronounced against P16_12 and P16_13, where neutralizing capacity fell to approximately 70 percent and 50 percent of that observed against the wild-type reference, respectively. Sera from individuals vaccinated with Gardasil likewise exhibited significantly diminished neutralization against P16_5, P16_12, and P16_13, dropping to roughly 60 percent of wild-type levels against P16_13, although Gardasil sera maintained full neutralization potency against P16_11, effectively compensating for the deficiency observed with Cervarix. In contrast, sera from Gardasil-9 recipients demonstrated broader resilience, successfully restoring neutralizing activity against P16_12 and improving the neutralization of P16_13 to approximately 80 percent of wild-type levels. Nevertheless, both P16_5 and P16_13 retained significant resistance across all three vaccines, maintaining reduced sensitivity even in the presence of multi-valent vaccine sera. To elucidate the structural and physical mechanisms responsible for the diminished neutralization of P16_5 and P16_13, the investigators conducted fifty-nanosecond all-atom molecular dynamics simulations using GROMACS with the CHARMM36m force field, modeling the variants in complex with the neutralizing monoclonal antibody Fab fragment H16.001 under physiological aqueous conditions. While the overall volume of the antigen-antibody complexes remained unchanged across variants and the minimum interatomic distances exhibited only minor shifts, root-mean-square deviation and root-mean-square fluctuation analyses demonstrated markedly heightened dynamic fluctuations in the antibody complexes formed with P16_5 and P16_13 compared to wild type, pointing to pronounced structural instability at the binding interface. These computational predictions were validated experimentally through modified enzyme-linked immunosorbent assays employing guanidine hydrochloride chaotropic elution to determine the avidity index of L1-specific serum IgG antibodies. The assays confirmed that polyclonal antibodies induced by all three vaccines bound to P16_5 and P16_13 with significantly lower avidity than to the reference wild type, demonstrating that accelerated complex dissociation impairs viral neutralization. To ascertain whether these immune-evading mutations emerged in response to vaccine deployment or through ancestral evolutionary processes, the authors performed positive selection analyses across the 228 unique L1 sequences by calculating nonsynonymous to synonymous substitution rate ratios using HyPhy software. The adaptive branch-site random effects likelihood model identified positive selection operating on 3.1 percent of phylogenetic branches. Furthermore, four discrete evolutionary models—MEME, FUBAR, FEL, and SLAC—concordantly confirmed that specific amino acid sites had undergone statistically significant positive selection. Specifically, residue N181, which is carried by variant P16_13, and residue A266, carried by variant P16_5, demonstrated robust evidence of positive selection across the predictive algorithms. Crucially, archival records show that P16_5 and P16_13 were isolated in 1994 and 1996, respectively, preceding the clinical introduction of the first prophylactic HPV vaccine in 2006. This chronological evidence proves that these mutational profiles evolved through natural, long-term adaptive host-pathogen interactions entirely independent of vaccine-induced selective pressure. The study highlights significant epidemiological implications, noting that variants P16_5 and P16_13 collectively account for approximately 17 percent of global HPV16 L1 prevalence in circulating populations. Sequence comparisons from P16_9 through P16_13 identified a recurring mutational signature spanning substitutions from H76Y to L474F, including T176N and T353P, suggesting that cumulative structural alterations across multiple surface residues drive antibody resistance rather than isolated point mutations. Discussing study limitations, the authors observed that while four variants failed to assemble pseudoviruses due to defective L1 expression or stability in human embryonic kidney cells, these sequences originated from cervical cancer biopsies where viral integration or unexamined interactions with native L2 variants might yield different biological behaviors in natural settings. Because current L1 virus-like particle vaccines remain indispensable for controlling cervical cancer and broader valency formulations provide enhanced cross-neutralization, the researchers concluded that sustained molecular surveillance of naturally circulating L1 variants is vital to anticipate ongoing evolutionary divergence and inform the composition of next-generation broad-spectrum vaccines. New Mathematical Model Uses Wavelets to Analyze Human Papillomavirus Dynamics - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A new mathematical model using the Lucas wavelet method was developed to analyze the transmission dynamics of human papillomavirus (HPV) and its progression to cervical cancer. The model divides the population into six compartments: susceptible, exposed, infected, those with cervical cancer, recovered, and vaccinated individuals. The study provides valuable insights into HPV transmission dynamics, offering quantitative tools for designing optimal control strategies for cervical cancer prevention. The research is in the publication phase and has not yet been peer-reviewed.

HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, November 28, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Technology New Mathematical Model Uses Wavelets to Analyze Human Papillomavirus Dynamics by November 24, 2026 in Technology Reading Time: 2 mins read 0 New Mathematical Model Uses Wavelets to Analyze Human Papillomavirus Dynamics Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A newly developed compartmental mathematical model uses the Lucas wavelet method to analyze the transmission dynamics of human papillomavirus and its progression to cervical cancer, according to research published by R. Yeshwanth and S. Kumbinarasaiah. The authors formulated a nonlinear system of differential equations designed to capture how human papillomavirus, recognized as the leading cause of cervical cancer affecting women worldwide, spreads and leads to malignancy across defined population groups. The mathematical framework divides the overall population into six distinct epidemiological compartments: susceptible individuals, exposed individuals, infected individuals, individuals who develop cervical cancer, recovered individuals, and vaccinated individuals. By defining these categories, the formulated nonlinear differential equation system models the specific transition pathways connecting the classes, explicitly incorporating the rates of initial infection, disease progression, clinical recovery, and disease-induced mortality. To solve the resulting nonlinear differential equations efficiently and accurately, the researchers implemented an approximate solution technique known as the Lucas wavelet method. The computational scheme utilizes Lucas wavelet basis functions that feature compact support and orthogonality. Through these mathematical properties, the method transforms the complex differential equation system into an algebraic system of equations suitable for rigorous computational evaluation. The authors conducted convergence analyses that confirmed the reliability and numerical stability of the proposed Lucas wavelet scheme. To demonstrate the operational efficiency of the method, the investigators carried out numerical simulations and generated graphical illustrations, directly comparing the performance and precision of the Lucas wavelet method against results obtained from various other computational techniques. Through graphical figures, the study evaluated and discussed how the model’s different dependent variables shift in response to variations in key epidemiological parameters. The authors report that these analytical findings provide valuable mathematical insight into human papillomavirus transmission dynamics, offering quantitative tools that can assist in designing optimal control strategies for cervical cancer prevention. 审查地图多步 宿主 网络指导 人乳头瘤病毒 进入和贩运 - Bioengineer.org

Review Maps Multistep Host Networks Directing Human Papillomavirus Entry and Trafficking - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报《病毒学杂志》上发表的一篇评论详细介绍了指导 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 进入和贩运的多步骤 宿主 网络。该研究强调了层粘连蛋白 332 和硫酸乙酰肝素蛋白聚糖等初始附着因子,以及亲环蛋白 B 和弗林蛋白酶等 宿主 因子,它们在病毒进入和细胞内运输中发挥着至关重要的作用。这项研究提供了一个框架,用于确定在早期阶段预防 HPV 感染的潜在目标,但没有明确说明具体的风险和影响。

Review Maps Multistep Host Networks Directing Human Papillomavirus Entry and Trafficking - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A review published in Virology Journal details the multistep host networks directing human papillomavirus (HPV) entry and trafficking. The study highlights initial attachment factors like laminin-332 and heparan sulfate proteoglycans, as well as host factors such as cyclophilin B and furin, which play crucial roles in the virus's entry and intracellular trafficking. This research provides a framework for identifying potential targets to prevent HPV infection at its earliest stages, though the specific risk and impact are not explicitly stated.

HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, November 28, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Biology Review Maps Multistep Host Networks Directing Human Papillomavirus Entry and Trafficking by November 22, 2026 in Biology Reading Time: 2 mins read 0 Review Maps Multistep Host Networks Directing Human Papillomavirus Entry and Trafficking Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Persistent infection with high-risk strains of human papillomavirus serves as a primary etiological cause of cervical cancer as well as multiple other epithelial malignancies. Because these persistent viral infections drive significant disease burdens, achieving an in-depth mechanistic understanding of early viral entry and subsequent intracellular trafficking remains critical for uncovering potential molecular targets that could block initial infection. In a review published in Virology Journal, the authors synthesize findings demonstrating that human papillomavirus entry is not a linear process mediated by a single receptor. Rather, the pathogen navigates an intricate, multistep trafficking network that sequentially traverses the basement membrane and extracellular matrix, the host plasma membrane, endosomal sorting bodies, Golgi-associated compartments, and ultimately the cell nucleus. In relatively well-characterized HPV16 and pseudovirion-based models, HPV virions can be captured and initially attached by basement membrane- or extracellular matrix-associated and cell-surface attachment factors. These attachment factors include laminin-332 and heparan sulfate proteoglycans. Discover more Book a Checkup Read Med Journals Find Cancer Care Check Symptoms Study Biology Subsequently, host factors such as cyclophilin B and furin participate in capsid conformational priming, L2 N-terminal exposure, and furin-mediated L2 cleavage. These steps create conditions for the transition of virions from initial attachment to entry platform assembly. The assembly of these entry platforms and the execution of atypical endocytosis engage a specialized array of host regulatory networks. The authors highlight contributions from integrin and focal adhesion kinase signaling pathways, tetraspanin-enriched microdomains, the annexin A2 and S100A10 heterotetramer complex, and various cytoskeleton-associated regulators. Nonetheless, the review emphasizes that the precise engagement points and relative functional dependence on these cellular components can differ depending on the specific viral genotype, the host cell lineage, and the experimental assays employed. After entry into the endosomal system, gamma-secretase-mediated L2 membrane insertion and Retromer- or Retriever-mediated retrograde trafficking are thought to promote delivery of the L2 and viral DNA complex to the nucleus. Ultimately, delivery of the L2 and viral DNA complex into the nucleus occurs through a vesicle-enclosed, mitosis-dependent import mechanism. By mapping these coordinated stages—encompassing initial membrane attachment platforms, non-canonical endocytic routes, endosomal sorting, retrograde transport routes, and cell-cycle-dependent nuclear entry—the authors delineate the comprehensive host-factor dependencies of human papillomavirus. These molecular intersections provide a framework for evaluating candidate cellular and viral targets for preventive or therapeutic interventions designed to arrest viral invasion at its earliest stages. Creative Diagnostics Introduces High-Risk HPV L1 Virus-Like Particles Produced In Yeast For Research And Assay Development - Menafn

News Brief
Creative Diagnostics has introduced a new product line of High-Risk HPV L1 VLPs produced in yeast for research and assay development. This product supports HPV research, serological assay development, and vaccine-related investigations by providing L1 VLP antigens that represent multiple high-risk HPV genotypes.

I agreeI declineChange my preferences Saturday 28 November 2026 02:04 GMT عربي Login Log in Remember Me Home News News by Industry News by Region American Europe Arab World Asia Africa RSS Press Distribution Press releases Submit Your Articles/Press Releases/Reports Pricing Market Data Equities Market Global Indices MENA Indices Qutoes & Charts End Of Day Stocks Currencies Currency Convertor Cross Rates Historical Currencies Libor Mena Stocks Commodoties Oil & Energy Economic Calender Research Premium Research Free Research Countries Saudi Arabia UAE Bahrain Qatar Kuwait Jordan Oman Egypt Lebanon Iraq Palestine Syria Tunisia Algeria Morocco Yemen Sections Events Financial Glossary USEuropeArabAsiaAfrica| PoliticsEconomyOil&EnergyEntertainmentSportFrenchSpanish Creative Diagnostics Introduces High-Risk HPV L1 Virus-Like Particles Produced In Yeast For Research And Assay Development Date: 2026-11-23 03:37:36 Share on Facebook Tweet on Twitter (MENAFN- ForPressRelease) Creative Diagnostics, a leading manufacturer and supplier of antibodies, antigens and assay kits, has launched a range of High-Risk HPV L1 VLPs Produced in Yeast, a portfolio of recombinant virus-like particle (VLP) antigens to support human papillomavirus (HPV) research, serological assay development, antibody evaluation, and vaccine-related investigations. This new product line provides researchers and laboratory professionals with access to L1 VLP antigens that represent multiple high-risk HPV genotypes, which can help to advance studies of HPV-specific immune responses and antigen recognition. HPV is a non-enveloped, double-stranded DNA virus with a T = 7 icosahedral capsid composed of 72 L1 pentamers (capsomeres), which are paired with L2 monomers. The L1 and L2 proteins have similar molecular weights (approximately 55kDa), with minor differences among different HPV genotypes. HPV has evolved a highly refined infection mechanism and exhibits specificity regarding the species, tissues and cell types it infects. Furthermore, the HPV virion enters the host via routes other than body fluids, resulting in a weak immune response from the host against neutralizing epitopes on the capsid. Given this low immune pressure, as well as the fact that the human antiviral immune response is primarily driven by type-specific neutralization, the HPV phylogeny is highly complex, with more than 200 distinct genotypes having accumulated to date. As there is currently no efficient method of extracting natural antigens from cultures, serological testing for HPV relies primarily on the use of“virus-like particles” (VLPs). When expressed via recombinant technology, the major HPV capsid protein L1 self-assembles into VLPs. These VLPs do not contain the viral genome, but they present type-specific conformational epitopes that are similar to those found in real viral particles. HPV L1 VLPs can induce high-titre, type-specific neutralizing antibodies and form the basis of current preventive vaccines. To better support scientific research in HPV immunology and diagnostics, Creative Diagnostics now provides researchers with high-risk HPV L1 VLPs produced in yeast, which are ideal for HPV-related research, diagnostic reagent development, antibody evaluation and viral antigen characterization. These antigens are based on the major capsid protein L1 of clinically significant high-risk HPV types. When properly expressed and processed, the L1 protein self-assembles into VLPs that resemble natural HPV capsids in structure but do not contain viral genomic material. This makes L1 VLPs non-infectious antigenic reagents that are particularly suitable for research that require the presentation of a natural capsid structure. Creative Diagnostics' new product portfolio covers nine high-risk genotypes relevant to cervical cancer research, HPV epidemiology, and immune response profiling, including HPV16, HPV18, HPV31, HPV33, HPV35, HPV45, HPV52, HPV59 and HPV68. This comprehensive testing panel enables the parallel comparison of subtype-specific antibody responses, cross-reactivity and antigenic performance across multiple high-risk genotypes. For researchers and assay developers who are interested in evaluating the High-Risk HPV L1 VLPs portfolio, product specifications, availability, application-specific requirements, or requesting bulk production, please visit About Creative Diagnostics Creative Diagnostics is a leading manufacturer and supplier of antibodies, viral antigens, innovative diagnostic components, and critical assay reagents. In addition to providing contract R&D and biologic manufacturing services for diagnostic manufacturers along with GMP biologics manufacturing for the biopharmaceutical market, the company aims to continue to act as a trusted source for all researchers' assay development and manufacturing needs. HPV 集成测试提高了 宫颈癌 分类的诊断准确性 - Bioengineer.org

HPV Integration Testing Improves Diagnostic Accuracy in Cervical Cancer Triage - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报A cross-sectional study at Fujian Maternity and Child Health Hospital in China demonstrated that detecting human papillomavirus chromosomal integration offers a highly accurate molecular biomarker for identifying severe precancerous cervical lesions in virus-positive women. The study involved 949 women and found 95.3 percent accuracy and 97.0 percent specificity for identifying cervical intraepithelial neoplasia grade 3 or worse, surpassing conventional liquid-based cytology. This method provides an objective molecular triage mechanism capable of refining clinical referrals and minimizing unnecessary invasive procedures.

HPV Integration Testing Improves Diagnostic Accuracy in Cervical Cancer Triage - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A cross-sectional study at Fujian Maternity and Child Health Hospital in China demonstrated that detecting human papillomavirus chromosomal integration offers a highly accurate molecular biomarker for identifying severe precancerous cervical lesions in virus-positive women. The study involved 949 women and found 95.3 percent accuracy and 97.0 percent specificity for identifying cervical intraepithelial neoplasia grade 3 or worse, surpassing conventional liquid-based cytology. This method provides an objective molecular triage mechanism capable of refining clinical referrals and minimizing unnecessary invasive procedures.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, November 28, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Cancer HPV Integration Testing Improves Diagnostic Accuracy in Cervical Cancer Triage by November 26, 2026 in Cancer Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 HPV Integration Testing Improves Diagnostic Accuracy in Cervical Cancer Triage Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A cross-sectional study within a major screening cohort demonstrates that detecting human papillomavirus chromosomal integration offers a highly accurate molecular biomarker for identifying severe precancerous cervical lesions in virus-positive women. Conducted across regional centers, the research evaluated whether identifying viral integration could resolve a critical dilemma in modern screening: distinguishing transient viral carriage from clinically dangerous pathology requiring colposcopy. In an analytical cohort of 949 women testing positive for high-risk human papillomavirus who underwent biopsy-confirmed histopathological assessment, viral integration testing demonstrated 95.3 percent accuracy and 97.0 percent specificity for identifying cervical intraepithelial neoplasia grade 3 or worse. These metrics substantially surpassed conventional liquid-based cytology, which registered 64.5 percent accuracy and 64.3 percent specificity. Crucially, the molecular assay detected high-grade disease without sacrificing clinical sensitivity, matching cytology at 66.7 percent versus 68.5 percent. These findings indicate that viral integration testing provides an objective molecular triage mechanism capable of refining clinical referrals and minimizing unnecessary invasive procedures. Cervical cancer represents a severe global health challenge, accounting for roughly 7 percent of female cancers with an estimated 660,000 incident diagnoses and over 340,000 deaths annually, with burdens concentrated disproportionately in low-resource settings. International guidelines recommend primary screening with high-risk human papillomavirus deoxyribonucleic acid testing because of its elevated diagnostic sensitivity. However, most viral infections are transient and naturally cleared by host immunity, creating a high volume of positive screening tests that complicate patient management. Current clinical algorithms rely heavily on liquid-based cytology, such as the ThinPrep cytology test, to triage virus-positive patients for colposcopy. Unfortunately, cytology demonstrates widely varying diagnostic performance, with reported sensitivity ranging from 55 percent to 94 percent and specificity between 63.8 percent and 75.5 percent. This morphological variability leads to frequent misclassification, unnecessary diagnostic biopsies, and overtreatment. Consequently, researchers have focused on viral genomic integration, a critical biological transition wherein viral deoxyribonucleic acid inserts into host chromosomes, disrupting genomic stability, elevating oncogene expression, and driving precancerous progression toward invasive carcinoma. Discover more Cancer Book a Checkup Check Symptoms To evaluate viral integration within routine clinical practice, researchers analyzed participants from the Fujian-Hubei Cervical Lesion Screening Cohort, which comprised 31,628 women presenting for screening. Between October 2021 and June 2024, investigators enrolled 1,326 women undergoing simultaneous primary screening and viral integration testing. Following the application of inclusion criteria, exactly 949 participants with valid integration results, definitive colposcopy-guided biopsies, and histopathological diagnoses formed the final analytic cohort. Ethical authorization was granted by the Institutional Review Board of Fujian Maternity and Child Health Hospital under approval identifier 2024KLR09009, with all participants providing written informed consent. Patients diagnosed with cervical intraepithelial neoplasia grade 3 or worse were managed under American Society for Colposcopy and Cervical Pathology consensus guidelines. Histopathological diagnoses served as the reference standard, categorized into benign or inflammatory findings, grade 1 neoplasia, grade 2 neoplasia, and grade 3 or worse. By requiring biopsy confirmation across all 949 participants, the investigators ensured an objective benchmark for verifying diagnostic accuracy. Discover more See an OB-GYN Find Cancer Care cancer cancers The testing protocol utilized commercial polymerase chain reaction reverse dot blot genotyping alongside high-throughput viral integration detection, known as HIVID. Genotyping categorized 23 types in exfoliated cervical cells, encompassing 13 high-risk types, five possible high-risk types, and five low-risk types. For integration testing, genomic deoxyribonucleic acid was extracted and captured via a multiplex probe system targeting entire viral genomes, followed by sequencing on an Illumina platform. To guarantee assay reproducibility, standardized spike-in reference fragments were added to each specimen, demanding a recovery rate of at least 0.01 percent for analytical validity, while sequencing quality maintained a Q30 score of 90.0 percent or higher. An automated bioinformatics pipeline classified specimens as integration-positive when at least six unique virus-host chimeric reads were detected at a specific genomic site, eliminating observer bias. Independent evaluations by two blinded pathologists determined histological status, resolving discrepancies through third-expert adjudication. Exclusions were applied to participants lacking definitive histological diagnoses or showing genotype discrepancies between testing platforms. Across the 949 evaluated participants, viral integration was detected in 6.6 percent, representing 63 individuals. The median age was 38 years in integration-negative women and 37 years in integration-positive women, showing no statistically significant age difference. In contrast, viral genotype distribution differed substantially: HPV16 and HPV18 appeared in 63.5 percent of integration-positive individuals compared to 29.0 percent of integration-negative patients. Genotype analysis identified integration in 28.3 percent of HPV18 infections, 11.5 percent of HPV16 infections, 4.4 percent of HPV52 infections, and 1.4 percent of HPV58 infections, with no integration observed in HPV45, 66, 68, 73, or 82. Viral integration rates correlated with cytological severity, increasing from 5.1 percent in normal cytology to 7.2 percent in atypical or low-grade cytology, and reaching 21.6 percent in high-grade squamous lesions. Pathological examination revealed that 57.1 percent of integration-positive participants harbored confirmed grade 3 or worse intraepithelial neoplasia, compared to just 2.0 percent among integration-negative women, confirming an association with advanced lesion severity. Multivariable logistic regression adjusted for age demonstrated that viral integration had the strongest association with cervical intraepithelial neoplasia grade 3 or worse, showing an adjusted odds ratio of 68.1 with a 95 percent confidence interval from 34.4 to 140.9. In contrast, non-HPV16/18 infections showed an adjusted odds ratio of 0.2, reflecting lower relative risk compared to HPV16/18. When evaluating infection patterns alongside integration, single infections with positive integration exhibited an adjusted odds ratio of 121.8, while multiple infections alone did not significantly alter risk relative to single infections. Sensitivity analyses confirmed the stability of these statistical models: bootstrap resampling with 10,000 iterations produced an adjusted odds ratio of 68.1 with a 95 percent bias-corrected confidence interval of 31.7 to 138.7, and Bayesian logistic regression yielded a posterior median odds ratio of 64.3 with a 95 percent credible interval of 32.7 to 130.4, mitigating concerns of model overfitting. Head-to-head diagnostic comparison revealed that viral integration testing significantly outperformed liquid-based cytology across multiple performance parameters for detecting grade 3 or worse lesions. Integration detection achieved a specificity of 97.0 percent compared to 64.3 percent for cytology, alongside an overall diagnostic accuracy of 95.3 percent versus 64.5 percent. The positive predictive value of integration testing reached 57.1 percent, more than five times higher than cytology at 10.4 percent. Diagnostic sensitivity remained comparable between the two modalities at 66.7 percent for integration testing and 68.5 percent for cytology. Subgroup analyses corroborated these findings: in HPV16/18-positive women, integration testing achieved a positive predictive value of 60.0 percent compared to 21.0 percent for cytology; in women older than 45 years, positive predictive value reached 38.9 percent versus 6.6 percent. Both approaches sustained high negative predictive values exceeding 97 percent, confirming low residual risk among test-negative patients. Evaluating combined diagnostic strategies showed that pairing viral integration testing with genotyping substantially improved diagnostic accuracy over existing clinical paradigms. Combining cytology with HPV16/18 genotyping yielded a sensitivity of 92.6 percent but exhibited a low specificity of 44.3 percent, an accuracy of 47.0 percent, and a positive predictive value of 9.1 percent. In contrast, combining viral integration detection with HPV16/18 genotyping significantly improved specificity to 69.5 percent and accuracy to 70.5 percent, while maintaining comparable sensitivity at 87.0 percent and raising the positive predictive value to 14.7 percent. Similarly, combining integration detection with cytology achieved 92.6 percent sensitivity and 64.0 percent accuracy, with 62.4 percent specificity. Across all combined modalities, negative predictive values remained high between 98.89 percent and 99.29 percent without statistically significant differences, demonstrating that incorporating integration detection sharpens risk assessment while maintaining high safety margins against missed high-grade lesions. Risk stratification demonstrated practical advantages for clinical decision-making. Women identified as integration-positive showed a 57.1 percent direct risk of cervical intraepithelial neoplasia grade 3 or worse, markedly exceeding the 10.4 percent risk observed among women with abnormal cytology. Conversely, integration-negative individuals accounted for 93.4 percent of the cohort and exhibited a low disease risk of 2.0 percent. Notably, women with normal cytology and negative integration status represented 59.2 percent of the entire cohort, with a disease risk of only 0.7 percent. This expands upon the standard low-risk cohort defined by the American Society for Colposcopy and Cervical Pathology—women with normal cytology who test negative for HPV16/18—which represented 42.1 percent of participants with a 1.0 percent risk. By reliably identifying a broader subset of low-risk patients, molecular integration triage could allow clinical systems to safely reduce surveillance intensity for the majority while prioritizing colposcopy for high-risk integration-positive individuals. The authors noted important methodological strengths alongside notable clinical limitations. Strengths included multivariable adjustment for key confounders, validation through bootstrap and Bayesian frameworks, and consistent subgroup performance across age categories and viral genotypes. However, the study cross-sectional design cannot determine the temporal sequence between integration events and lesion development, while the absence of longitudinal data precludes causal inferences regarding disease progression. Furthermore, the overall integration positivity rate of 6.6 percent and low regional prevalence for genotypes such as HPV45 limited sample sizes for specific subgroups, preventing firm conclusions regarding rare genotypes. The financial cost of high-throughput integration testing also remains higher than routine genotyping, and this investigation did not perform a formal health-economic analysis. The researchers concluded that larger longitudinal studies are necessary to evaluate cost-effectiveness, establish standardized laboratory protocols, and validate integration testing as an objective triage tool in primary screening programs. View five news elements
Time
Conducted across regional centers, the research evaluated whether identifying viral integration could resolve a critical dilemma in modern screening: distinguishing transient viral carriage from clinically dangerous pathology requiring colposcopy.
Location and population
Fujian Maternity and Child Health Hospital, China.
Event
A cross-sectional study demonstrated that detecting human papillomavirus chromosomal integration offers a highly accurate molecular biomarker for identifying severe precancerous cervical lesions in virus-positive women.
Scale, impact and risk
The study found 95.3 percent accuracy and 97.0 percent specificity for identifying cervical intraepithelial neoplasia grade 3 or worse, surpassing conventional liquid-based cytology. The study involved 949 women and showed that viral integration testing provides an objective molecular triage mechanism capable of refining clinical referrals and minimizing unnecessary invasive procedures.
Response, status and uncertainty
The findings indicate that viral integration testing can improve diagnostic accuracy in cervical cancer triage, but further investigation and implementation are needed.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

较高的 HPV 病毒载量与 宫颈癌 风险增加相关

Higher HPV viral loads tied to increased risk of cervical cancer
新闻简报发表在《癌症》杂志上的一项研究发现,较高的 HPV 病毒载量与 宫颈癌 风险增加有关。这项研究由南京医科大学的张丽娜博士及其同事领导,追踪了 624 名持续感染 HPV 的女性,其中 471 名女性已清除病毒,75 名女性感染重新激活。病毒载量持续较高的女性有 90% 的可能性出现宫颈癌前病变,而稳定载量较低的女性和中度下降轨迹的女性分别为 11% 和 18%。研究结果强调了监测病毒载量随时间变化的重要性,以更好地评估未来风险并改进 监测 策略。然而,作者警告说,其他因素也可能影响结果,并且该研究是观察性的而不是实验性的。研究结果可能会导致针对宫颈疾病预防制定更加个性化的后续计划。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于 2025 年发表,该杂志的出版商 Wiley 在新闻稿中报道了研究结果。分析的数据反映了 2025 例麻疹病例和 疫苗 覆盖数据。
地点与对象
该研究重点关注美国 30 个州(占美国人口的 71.6%)以及 2,214 个县中的 1,821 个县。该研究涉及 624 名持续感染 HPV 的女性、471 名已清除 HPV 感染的女性以及 75 名重新激活感染的女性。
事件
发表在《癌症》杂志上的一项研究发现,较高的 HPV 病毒载量与 宫颈癌 风险增加相关。该研究追踪了病毒载量随时间的变化,并将其与宫颈病变的发展联系起来。
规模、影响与风险
在 624 名持续感染 HPV 的女性中,11% 的病毒载量稳定较低,18% 的病毒载量中度下降,74% 的病毒载量适度增加,90% 的病毒载量持续较高,后者出现宫颈癌前病变的可能性最高。
应对、状态与不确定性
研究结果表明,监测病毒载量变化可以改善 宫颈癌 预防策略。然而,作者指出,病毒载量与 宫颈癌 风险之间的关系可能会受到其他因素的影响,并且结果基于观察数据而不是实验方法。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Higher HPV viral loads tied to increased risk of cervical cancer

News Brief
A study published in the journal Cancer found that higher HPV viral loads are linked to an increased risk of cervical cancer. The research, led by Dr. Lina Zhang and colleagues at Nanjing Medical University, tracked 624 women with persistent HPV infection, 471 who cleared it, and 75 with reactivated infection. Women with persistently high viral loads had a 90% likelihood of developing precancerous cervical lesions, compared to 11% with low stable loads and 18% with moderate declining trajectories. The findings highlight the importance of monitoring viral load changes over time to better assess future risk and improve surveillance strategies. However, the authors caution that other factors may also influence outcomes, and the study is observational rather than experimental. The results could lead to more personalized follow-up plans for cervical disease prevention.
Original source text (English)

Topics & Projects Antimicrobial Stewardship Chronic Wasting Disease Osterholm Update Podcasts Newsletters About CIDRAP CIDRAP in the News Our Staff Our Director Osterholm in the Press Shop Merchandise Contact Us About Search CLOSE Search Support Menu Higher HPV viral loads tied to increased risk of cervical cancer News brief Meghan Holohan Topics Human Papillomavirus (HPV) Share Copied to clipboard ronnachaipark / iStock Most people will experience a human papillomavirus (HPV) infection in their lifetime. High-risk strains of HPV cause cervical cancer in women, the fourth most common cancer in women worldwide. A paper published this week in the journal Cancer finds that viral load contributes to why some people develop cervical cancer, and others don’t. “Two women with similar HPV viral loads at one visit may have very different outcomes depending on how those levels change over time,” said co-corresponding author Lina Zhang, MD, in a news release from Wiley, the publisher of the journal. “Our findings suggest that a rising or persistently high viral load may signal a greater likelihood of cervical lesions.” Findings could lead to better monitoring Zhang and her colleagues at Nanjing Medical University in China led the study, which tracked 624 women (53.3%) who had persistent HPV infection, 471 (40.3%) who cleared their HPV infection, and 75 (6.4%) with a reactivated infection. Women with lingering HPV infections had higher viral loads than those who cleared it or had HPV reactivation. Those with moderate increasing or persistently high viral loads were more likely to develop cervical cancer than those with low stable or moderate declining viral loads. In total, 11% of women with low stable viral loads, 18% of women with moderate declining trajectories, 74% with moderately increasing viral loads, and 90% with persistently high viral loads had precancerous cervical lesions. Two women with similar HPV viral loads at one visit may have very different outcomes depending on how those levels change over time. Lina Zhang, MD Considering viral load could help doctors understand a woman’s future risk and create better surveillance plans for them. “We hope these findings will contribute to more personalized follow-up strategies and ultimately improve cervical disease prevention,” said co–corresponding author Zhonghua Shi, MD, in the release. Nonmedical measles vaccine exemptions for kindergarteners tied to 16% higher risk of outbreaks News brief Mary Van Beusekom, MS Topics Measles Childhood Vaccines NIH Higher rates of nonmedical measles-vaccine exemptions for school entry were associated with a 16% higher outbreak likelihood in a study representing 237 million people in 30 US states. For the study, published yesterday in JAMA Network Open, a team led by a University of Washington researcher analyzed measles cases reported by the Johns Hopkins University Measles Tracker in 2025, state- and county-level data on measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine coverage among kindergarteners, and vaccine exemptions. The outbreak proxy was at least three measles cases per county, a count threshold rather than per-capita incidence; counties below that level were retained. The threshold didn’t establish epidemiologic linkage. The secondary outcome consisted of total county cases in counties with at least three cases rather than cases per outbreak. “Measles is among the most transmissible human pathogens, so even small, geographically clustered immunity gaps can sustain local transmission,” the authors wrote. “As aggregate national and state measles-mumps-rubella (MMR) coverage can obscure this local susceptibility, pockets of unvaccinated persons may persist even where aggregate coverage appears adequate.” Findings may guide school preparedness The analytic sample represented 71.6% of the US population, and 1,821 of 2,214 (82.2%) counties reported measles cases in 2025; 3.4% met the proxy. Average MMR coverage was 91.1%, and the average nonmedical exemption rate was 4.6%. Routinely collected exemption data may complement local knowledge when prioritizing school-level review and preparedness. The joint model showed a link between greater exemption rates and outbreak occurrence (odds ratio [OR], 1.16) but no association for MMR uptake (OR, 1.01). In the exploratory burden model, the incidence rate ratio was 1.08. Higher exemption rates were tied to lower MMR coverage. Joint-model ORs were 1.12 to 1.17 using alternative thresholds. Burden estimates remained positive but were less consistent in sensitivity analyses, with wide confidence intervals that indicated imprecision. The relationship between school nonmedical exemption rates and a higher likelihood of measles outbreaks may reflect clustering of susceptibility, differential measurement error in uptake and exemption data, or both, the authors said. “Routinely collected exemption data may complement local knowledge when prioritizing school-level review and preparedness,” they concluded. Point-of-care test for syphilis receives emergency use authorization News brief Chris Dall, MA Topics Sexually Transmitted Infections Diagnostics quantic69 / iStock The US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has issued emergency use authorization for a point-of-care molecular test for active syphilis, and three other pathogens that cause sexually transmitted infections (STIs). The test, developed by Roche Diagnostics, can detect Treponema pallidum—the bacterium that causes syphilis—from a single lesion swab sample within 15 minutes. It can also detect mpox virus and herpes simplex virus types 1 and 2. The three STIs can cause similar symptoms but require different treatments. Officials with the National Institutes of Health (NIH), which helped speed the review of the test through its Rapid Acceleration of Diagnostics Tech Independent Test Assessment Program, say the point-of-care test can help healthcare providers identify active syphilis infection and initiate appropriate treatment more quickly. “Point-of-care testing leads to faster treatment in the same visit, thus reducing transmission and worsening illness,” Bruce Tromberg, PhD, director of NIH’s National Institute of Biomedical Imaging and Bioengineering, said in an agency press release. Rise in syphilis cases It’s the first point-of-care test for syphilis to receive FDA authorization. Current syphilis diagnosis relies on blood-based serologic tests. US syphilis cases have risen by 42% since 2020, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, with 190,242 cases reported in 2024. Cases of congenital syphilis—the transfer of the infection from the mother to the fetus—have risen by 82%. If left untreated, syphilis can cause serious heart and brain damage, blindness, deafness, and paralysis. Congenital syphilis can lead to miscarriage, infant death, and lifelong medical issues. NIH says the test will become available in the United States in the coming months. RSV shots safe for older adults, alone or in combo, analysis finds News brief Liz Szabo, MA Topics Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV) Adult (non-flu) Vaccines lucigerma / iStock Multiple clinical trials have found that respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) vaccines are safe in adults. Now, a new study in people age 60 and older shows it’s also safe to receive an RSV shot plus another vaccine in the same week. In a study of 18,252 people published this week in Vaccine, less than 1% of participants reported seeking medical attention during the week after being vaccinated against RSV. The study was based on reports to V-safe, a voluntary registry run by the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) that allows people to report side effects or other problems after receiving a vaccine. About one-third of people reported a mild or moderate health issue within a week after getting an RSV shot, including a sore arm, fatigue, muscle ache, and headache. About one in three people vaccinated against RSV received another vaccine within a week, according to the study, led by CDC scientists. Side effects differ by vaccine manufacturer People given the RSV vaccine made by Pfizer (Abrysvo) plus another shot were more likely to report side effects around the injection site compared with people who received the Pfizer shot alone. Study participants who received GSK’s RSV vaccine (Arexvy), either alone or with another vaccine, were more likely to report symptoms than those who received the Pfizer vaccine. Those who received the Moderna RSV vaccine (mResvia) had a similar risk of side effects as those given the Pfizer shot. The CDC recommends that people age 75 and older receive a single RSV shot. People older than 60 may also get an RSV vaccine if they are at high risk of developing severe disease from the illness. Uptick in H5N1 avian flu activity continues across North America News brief Stephanie Soucheray, MA Topics Avian Influenza (Bird Flu) Addison Berry/Flickr cc Avian flu activity continues to rise across the nation, with two more detections noted in dairy cattle herds this week, one each in Texas and Washington state. In the past 30 days, 26 cattle detections have been noted in dairy herds by the US Department of Agriculture’s Animal and Plant Health Inspection Service (APHIS). Of the 26 detections, 17 have been in Washington state, with high activity in major dairy operations in the Yakima Valley. This week the World Health Organization detailed three human H5N1 cases reported in Yakima Valley in October and November, stating that all three patients were adults who worked on dairy cattle farms. No cases involved person-to-person transmission, and the report does not say if infected people all worked on the same farm. One of the case-patients (who had underlying health problems) died from the infection and was the first death reported globally following exposure to infected dairy cattle. The other two patients had mild illness, with conjunctivitis as an initial symptom. Zoo outbreak kills spider monkey, tigrillo In commercial poultry news, five new operations have reported H5N1 outbreaks this week, including a commercial broiler production in Sussex County, Delaware, affecting 269,900 birds. In the past 30 days, 4.02 million birds have been hit with avian flu in 32 US flocks, including 10 commercial and 22 backyard flocks, according to APHIS data. Finally in international news, Mexican health authorities announced the immediate closure of the Leon Municipal Zoo in the state of Guanajuato following an outbreak of H5N1. On November 16, the zoo noted the virus in dead migratory waterfowl found in the zoo’s central lake. Yesterday, testing showed H5N1 infection in two dead captive animals: a tigrillo (a small member of the cat family; see photo above) and a spider monkey, with a second spider monkey showing symptoms. The zoo is closed for the next five days. CWD digest: Fatal disease found on deer farms in Michigan, Kentucky News brief Mary Van Beusekom, MS Topics Chronic Wasting Disease Larry Smith / Flickr cc Michigan: The Michigan Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (MDARD) has confirmed the first-time detection of chronic wasting disease (CWD) in a cervid on a Roscommon County farm. While the type of cervid wasn’t identified in the news release, the farm raises game animals such as deer, elk, and reindeer, per media reports. Roscommon County, in the north-central part of Michigan’s Lower Peninsula, borders CWD-positive Ogemaw and Gladwin counties. “The presence of CWD in farmed cervid facilities is not new to Michigan; prior to this detection, cases have been found across multiple counties, including Calhoun, Hillsdale, Kent, Lake, Mackinac, Mecosta, Montcalm, Newaygo, and Osceola counties,” MDARD said. CWD is transmitted through infectious misfolded proteins called prions, which spread from animal to animal or through environmental contamination. Kentucky: A Shelby County deer farm is under quarantine after a deer tested positive for CWD last week—the first case in the county, the Kentucky Department of Agriculture reports. Shelby County is in the north-central part of the state and doesn’t border any CWD-positive county. “The Office of State Veterinarian established a management (5 mile radius) and surveillance (15 mile radius) zone for herds near the affected deer farm” and “is diligently reviewing recent movement records, and all deer farms in the management and surveillance zones are under restricted movement until further order of the State Veterinarian,” the news release said.
